Energy solutions of singular SPDEs on Hilbert spaces with applications to domains with boundary conditions
Authors: Lukas Gräfner, Nicolas Perkowski, Shyam Popat
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
In this paper we extend the theory of energy solutions for singular SPDEs, focusing on equations driven by highly irregular noise with bilinear nonlinearities, including scaling critical examples. By introducing Gelfand triples and leveraging infinite-dimensional analysis in Hilbert spaces together with an integration by parts formula under the invariant measure, we largely eliminate the need for Fourier series and chaos expansions. This approach broadens the applicability of energy solutions to a wider class of SPDEs, offering a unified treatment of various domains and boundary conditions. Our examples are motivated by recent work on scaling limits of interacting particle systems.

Introduction

The semigroup approach to SPDEs in Hilbert spaces, as developed in the seminal monograph by Da Prato and Zabczyk [11] is based on infinite-dimensional analysis in Hilbert spaces combined with stochastic analysis and martingale techniques. It offers a unified approach to a wide range of equations driven by infinite-dimensional Wiener processes. The semigroup approach is notable for its versatility, addressing not only well-posedness but also more advanced aspects including martingale problems and connections to infinite-dimensional Kolmogorov equations [4, 10], study of invariant measures and ergodicity [9], stability and dynamic properties, control theory, numerical analysis, regularization by noise, reflected equations, or scaling limits. This generality has made the semigroup approach an invaluable tool for researchers tackling both theoretical and applied problems in infinite-dimensional stochastic systems.

However, for certain classes of SPDEs, particularly those involving highly singular noise and nonlinearities, the traditional semigroup approach breaks down. When the noise becomes too irregular, the solution may no longer take values in a space on which the nonlinearity is well-defined. In this case, renormalization becomes essential, and a new conceptual framework is required to handle the resulting singularities. This gap has been filled by the development of singular SPDE theory, as pioneered by Hairer’s theory of regularity structures [27] and Gubinelli-Imkeller-Perkowski’s paracontrolled calculus [16], both originating in Lyons’s rough path theory [30]. These pathwise techniques introduced the idea of scaling subcriticality, where the nonlinearities become small at small scales. This enables a perturbative expansion of the solution around the linearized equation that gains regularity, in the spirit of previous works by Da Prato and Debussche [3, 5]. Singular SPDEs grew into a vibrant new area of research in SPDEs. But despite the impressive progress in the last decade, the probabilistic structure of singular SPDEs is still worse understood than for SPDEs on Hilbert spaces in the semigroup approach. In particular, the pathwise nature of these methods complicates the study of martingale properties or the relation to infinite-dimensional Kolmogorov equations, and the connection to the broader probabilistic methods used in the semigroup approach is largely absent in the existing theories of singular SPDEs.

This is where energy solutions become relevant. Energy solutions offer a probabilistic alternative for certain singular SPDEs with a tractable probabilistic structure. They provide a martingale-based theory that is compatible with Itô calculus. Energy solutions were first defined and constructed by Gonçalves and Jara [18] for the stochastic Burgers equation in the context of scaling limits for particle systems. Gubinelli and Jara [17] found a slightly stronger formulation and extended the construction to stochastic Navier-Stokes equations and fractional Burgers equations. In both of these works, the question of uniqueness remained open. For the stochastic Burgers equation, Gubinelli and Perkowski [20] later proved well-posedness with the help of the Cole-Hopf transform that linearizes the equation. In a subsequent work [21] they extended the well-posedness theory to other equations including the fractional stochastic Burgers equation in the entire subcritical regime, by leveraging a martingale problem approach. Notably, they constructed the infinitesimal generator and solved the infinite-dimensional Kolmogorov backward equation by combining Gaussian analysis with Fourier series computations. More recently, the ongoing Ph.D. thesis of Gräfner [24] has advanced the theory of energy solutions by adopting a more systematic functional analytic approach that largely removes the need for Fourier series computations. This approach relies on the chaos expansion under a Gaussian or non-Gaussian reference measure, and it is able to handle scaling critical equations like the critical fractional stochastic Burgers equation and the stochastic surface quasi-geostrophic equation. It also allows for non-stationary perturbations of drift and diffusion, time-dependent coefficients, and it provides a PDE approach (compared to the semigroup approach used here) for the Kolmogorov backward equation, as well as Markov selection results for some scaling super-critical equations.

Our current work strengthens the theory of energy solutions, broadening its scope and enhancing its robustness. We build upon the foundational ideas of SPDEs on Hilbert spaces [11] and combine them with the Gelfand triples from the variational approach to SPDEs [29] to rigorously define the operators involved in the stochastic dynamics, also inspired by the construction of singular operators like the Anderson Hamiltonian in the context of singular SPDEs [1]. We consider densely embedded Hilbert spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\subset H \subset V^*$$\end{document}V⊂H⊂V∗ and our focus is on equations of the form1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t u = - A u + {:} B (u, u) {:} + \sqrt{2 A} \xi . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tu=-Au+:B(u,u):+2Aξ.where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:V\rightarrow V^*$$\end{document}A:V→V∗ is a symmetric dissipative operator, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B: V\otimes _s H\rightarrow V^*$$\end{document}B:V⊗sH→V∗ (the tensor product is defined later) is a bilinear operator with certain symmetry and antisymmetry properties, : B : represents renormalization, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ is a space-time white noise on H. The candidate invariant measure for this equation is the law of the white noise on H, which in infinite dimensions is supported only on a larger Hilbert space than H [11]. Therefore, some care is needed to interpret the (renormalized) nonlinearity : B : . We show that B can be interpreted as a distribution on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mu )$$\end{document}L2(μ) and we use regularization by noise techniques, in particular infinite-dimensional semigroups/Kolmogorov equations, to prove weak well-posedness of energy solutions to (1). This is in the spirit of works by Da Prato et al on infinite-dimensional regularization by noise [6–8]. A crucial ingredient is the energy estimate of energy solutions, see Definition 2.18, which serves as a selection principle guaranteeing the uniqueness (see also the discussion on the selection principle in [23]).

One of our main contributions is the introduction of Gelfand triples in this context, eliminating the need for Fourier techniques1 while extending the theory to accommodate a wide range of domains, boundary conditions and operator types. Our formulation is motivated by potential applications to the scaling limits of particle systems, inspired in part by recent work on SPDEs with regional fractional Laplacians as scaling limits by Cardoso and Gonçalves [2]. On the technical side, we mostly avoid the use of chaos expansions that are prominent in [24], and instead rely on an integration by parts rule for the reference measure. This choice not only enhances robustness but also simplifies the necessary estimates.

Remark 1.1

The assumptions on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ A $$\end{document}A and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ B $$\end{document}B are natural, while the assumption on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (\rho _N) $$\end{document}(ρN) is more technical. It may be possible to avoid this assumption by finding different arguments. However, in concrete examples, there often exists a natural family of approximations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (\rho _N) $$\end{document}(ρN) for which the estimates in Assumption 1 hold. For instance, in the derivation of energy solutions from particle systems, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \rho _N $$\end{document}ρN is frequently defined by integrating against a rescaled indicator function over a small set, see [2, 18, 25].

Our aim is to prove uniqueness and, under additional assumptions, existence of energy solutions to the equation11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t u = - A u + {:}B (u, u){:} + \sqrt{2 A} \xi , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tu=-Au+:B(u,u):+2Aξ,where : B(u, u) : is a suitable renormalization of the bilinearity, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ is a space-time white noise over H, i.e. a centered Gaussian process indexed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2 (\mathbb {R}_+; H)$$\end{document}L2(R+;H) with covariance\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbb {E} [\xi (g) \xi (h)] = \int _0^{\infty } \langle g (t), h (t) \rangle \textrm{d}t. $$\end{document}E[ξ(g)ξ(h)]=∫0∞⟨g(t),h(t)⟩dt.We analyze the equation under the candidate invariant measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ, the law of the white noise on H. Under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ the process \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\eta (h))_{h \in H}$$\end{document}(η(h))h∈H is centered Gaussian with covariance\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int \eta (h) \eta (g) \mu (\textrm{d}\eta ) = \langle h, g \rangle _H . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫η(h)η(g)μ(dη)=⟨h,g⟩H.Energy solutions are weak (both analytically and probabilistically) solutions that respect the natural probabilistic structure of (11); see Definition 2.18 for a precise formulation.

Remark 1.2

The measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ makes sense as a Gaussian probability measure on any Hilbert space W such that there is a dense Hilbert-Schmidt embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota : H \hookrightarrow W $$\end{document}ι:H↪W since then we can consider the unique Gaussian measure with trace-class operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota \iota ^{*}$$\end{document}ιι∗ on W. Such an embedding always exists and the specific choice of W does not matter since we deal with a W-valued random variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η for which the law of the process \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\langle \eta , \iota (h) \rangle _W)_{h \in H}$$\end{document}(⟨η,ι(h)⟩W)h∈H is uniquely determined, see e.g. [11, Sec. 4.1.2].

Theorem 1.3

(Existence of energy solutions, Theorem 3.5 below) Suppose that S is a nuclear Fréchet space and that there exists a complete orthonormal system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(e_n)_{n \in \mathbb {N}} \subset V$$\end{document}(en)n∈N⊂V of H consisting of eigenvectors for A with eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda _n)_{n \in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(λn)n∈N. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu \ll \mu $$\end{document}ν≪μ be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\textrm{d}\nu }{\textrm{d}\mu } \in L^2(\mu )$$\end{document}dνdμ∈L2(μ). Then under Assumptions 1 and 1, there exists an energy solution u to (11) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0 \sim \nu $$\end{document}u0∼ν.

Theorem 1.4

(Uniqueness of energy solutions, Theorem 3.7 and Remark 3.8 below) Suppose Assumptions 1 and 1 are satisfied. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu \ll \mu $$\end{document}ν≪μ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\textrm{d}\nu }{\textrm{d}\mu } \in L^2 (\mu )$$\end{document}dνdμ∈L2(μ) and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_t)_{t \geqslant 0}$$\end{document}(ut)t⩾0 be an energy solution to (11) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0 \sim \nu $$\end{document}u0∼ν. Then the law of u is uniquely determined by its initial condition, u is a Markov process and it has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ as an invariant measure. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert v\Vert _{\dot{V}}=0$$\end{document}‖v‖V˙=0 only for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=0 \in V$$\end{document}v=0∈V, then u is ergodic.

Remark 1.5

(Normalization of coefficients) We chose very specific coefficients for convenience, but by a small adaptation we can treat the equation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \partial _t u = - \nu A u + \lambda {:} B (u, u) {:} + \sqrt{\sigma ^2 2 A} \xi , $$\end{document}∂tu=-νAu+λ:B(u,u):+σ22Aξ,for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu , \sigma ^2 > 0$$\end{document}ν,σ2>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}λ∈R. In that case we simply redefine \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{A} :=\nu A$$\end{document}A~:=νA and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{B} = \lambda B$$\end{document}B~=λB and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\xi } = \sqrt{\frac{\sigma ^2}{\nu }} \xi $$\end{document}ξ~=σ2νξ to obtain an equation with normalized coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t u = - \tilde{A} u + : \tilde{B} (u, u) : + \sqrt{2 \tilde{A}} \tilde{\xi }$$\end{document}∂tu=-A~u+:B~(u,u):+2A~ξ~. The noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\xi }$$\end{document}ξ~ is no longer a space-time white noise over H, but over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{H} = H$$\end{document}H~=H with rescaled norm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert h \Vert _{\tilde{H}}^2 = \frac{\sigma ^2}{\nu } \Vert h \Vert _H^2$$\end{document}‖h‖H~2=σ2ν‖h‖H2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert v \Vert _{\tilde{V}}^2 = \frac{\sigma ^2}{\nu } \Vert v \Vert _V^2$$\end{document}‖v‖V~2=σ2ν‖v‖V2. The operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{A}$$\end{document}A~ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{B}$$\end{document}B~ satisfy similar estimates with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{V}$$\end{document}V~ as A and B do with respect to V. Therefore, the same existence and uniqueness results hold in that case.

Remark 1.6

All results of this paper, in particular the uniqueness statements, extend to the equation obtained by adding a linear term Cu to the drift in (11), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C:\dot{V}\rightarrow V^*$$\end{document}C:V˙→V∗ is bounded, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C(S)\subset H$$\end{document}C(S)⊂H, and C is antisymmetric on S, i.e.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle Cf,g\rangle = -\langle Cg,f\rangle , \qquad f,g\in S $$\end{document}⟨Cf,g⟩=-⟨Cg,f⟩,f,g∈S(equivalently \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle Cf,f\rangle =0$$\end{document}⟨Cf,f⟩=0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in S$$\end{document}f∈S). In the weak formulation (47) this corresponds to the additional term\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathrm d u_t(f)=\cdots - Hence, it is a bounded antisymmetric perturbation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_0$$\end{document}L0 and can be handled by a simpler variant of the arguments used for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}G.

Remark 1.7

In the existence result Theorem 1.3, the condition that A has an eigenbasis is technical and can likely be omitted. A possible workaround would be to approximate a self-adjoint A satisfying Assumption (A) by the spectral discretizations\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ A^N := \sum _{i=1}^\infty \frac{i}{N}\, {\textbf{1}}_{\bigl (\frac{i-1}{N},\frac{i}{N}\bigr ]}(A), $$\end{document}AN:=∑i=1∞iN1(i-1N,iN](A),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{1}}_I(A)$$\end{document}1I(A) denotes a spectral projection (functional calculus). Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^N$$\end{document}AN is self-adjoint and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (A^N)\subset \frac{1}{N}{\mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}σ(AN)⊂1NN, hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^N$$\end{document}AN is diagonalizable and Theorem 3.5 yields an energy solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u^N$$\end{document}uN for the equation with A replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^N$$\end{document}AN. To pass to a limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}N→∞ one would need uniformity of the estimates presented below, in particular the tightness bounds of Lemma 3.4. This is in the spirit of Gubinelli–Turra [26], who approximate the Laplacian on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^2$$\end{document}R2 by Laplacians on tori of increasing size.

Structure of the paper

The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 we provide a functional analytic setup for our problem. Section 2.1 is dedicated to deriving expressions for the operators appearing in the Itô formula for energy solutions, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_0$$\end{document}L0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}G, corresponding to the linear part of the dynamics and the bilinear operator B as in (11) respectively. In Section 2.2 we derive bounds for the operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_0$$\end{document}L0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}G on Sobolev type spaces based on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mu )$$\end{document}L2(μ). Finally in Section 2.3 we define energy solutions in Definition 2.18 and give an alternative characterization in Theorem 2.20 that is more applicable in the particle systems setting. Section 3 is dedicated to the construction of energy solutions and the corresponding semigroups. In Section 3.1 we construct the semigroup and resolvent. Energy solutions are constructed in Section 3.2. Finally, in Section 3.3 we prove the uniqueness of energy solutions in Theorem 3.7, by establishing duality between semigroup and energy solution.

The final section is devoted to examples. For simplicity we focus on the one-dimensional Burgers equation with various linear operators and boundary conditions. In Section 4 we provide a detailed analysis for the (multi-component) stochastic Burgers equation on (0, 1) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0, \infty )$$\end{document}(0,∞) with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions, possibly with a divergence form differential operator instead of the usual Laplacian. We also discuss homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions, which we can handle in a hyperviscous setting with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = (1 - \Delta )^{\theta }$$\end{document}A=(1-Δ)θ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta > 1$$\end{document}θ>1. In the Dirichlet case we can also choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$- A$$\end{document}-A as the regional fractional Laplacian of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \left[ \frac{3}{2}, 2 \right) $$\end{document}γ∈32,2, which is motivated by recent results on fluctuations in interacting particle systems by [2].

Functional analytic setup

Derivation of the generator

In this section we derive useful expressions for the operators appearing in the Itô formula for energy solutions. We work on the probability space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =W$$\end{document}Ω=W with W as in Remark 1.2, where we consider the coordinate map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta :\Omega \rightarrow W$$\end{document}η:Ω→W, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (\omega ) = \omega $$\end{document}η(ω)=ω, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (h):=\langle \eta ,\mathcal \iota (h)\rangle _W$$\end{document}η(h):=⟨η,ι(h)⟩W, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in H$$\end{document}h∈H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}} = \sigma (\eta (h):h \in H)$$\end{document}F=σ(η(h):h∈H). We write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ for the measure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Omega , {\mathcal {F}})$$\end{document}(Ω,F) under which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η is a white noise on H, see Remark 1.2.

Definition 2.1

(Cylinder functions) Any cylinder function is of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F (\eta ) = \Phi (\eta (f))$$\end{document}F(η)=Φ(η(f)), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in S^m$$\end{document}f∈Sm and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi : \mathbb {R}^m \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}Φ:Rm→R is a polynomial. We write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C for the set of all cylinder functions.

For a cylinder function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F (\eta ) = \Phi (\eta (f))$$\end{document}F(η)=Φ(η(f)) we define the operator12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}_0 F (\eta ) :=\sum _{i = 1}^m \partial _i \Phi (\eta (f)) \eta (- A f_i) + \frac{1}{2} \sum _{i, j = 1}^m \partial _{i j}^2 \Phi (\eta (f)) 2 \langle A f_i, f_j \rangle _H. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L0F(η):=∑i=1m∂iΦ(η(f))η(-Afi)+12∑i,j=1m∂ij2Φ(η(f))2⟨Afi,fj⟩H.Formally, this is the differential operator that appears when we apply Itô’s formula to the (weak formulation of the) linear part of the dynamics (11) (i.e. with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B = 0$$\end{document}B=0). To proceed, we need the following well known observation (see [32] for the definitions of D, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ):

Lemma 2.2

(Multiplication formula for Skorokhod integral) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ be the Skorokhod integral and let D be the Malliavin derivative, both with respect to the white noise on H. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \mathcal {C}$$\end{document}F∈C and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in \operatorname {dom} (\delta )$$\end{document}φ∈dom(δ). Then13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F \delta (\varphi ) = \delta (F \varphi ) + \langle D F, \varphi \rangle _H . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Fδ(φ)=δ(Fφ)+⟨DF,φ⟩H.In particular, we have for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in H$$\end{document}h∈H14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F (\eta ) \eta (h) = \delta (F h) (\eta ) + \langle D F (\eta ), h \rangle _H = \delta (F h) (\eta ) + D_h F (\eta ) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(η)η(h)=δ(Fh)(η)+⟨DF(η),h⟩H=δ(Fh)(η)+DhF(η).

Proof

Let G be another cylinder function. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} [(F \delta (\varphi ) - \langle D F, \varphi \rangle _H) G]&= \mathbb {E} [\delta (\varphi ) F G] -\mathbb {E} [\langle D F, \varphi \rangle _H G]\\&= \mathbb {E} [\langle \varphi G, D F \rangle _H] +\mathbb {E} [\langle \varphi F, D G \rangle _H] -\mathbb {E} [\langle D F, \varphi \rangle _H G]\\&= \mathbb {E} [\delta (\varphi F) G] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[(Fδ(φ)-⟨DF,φ⟩H)G]=E[δ(φ)FG]-E[⟨DF,φ⟩HG]=E[⟨φG,DF⟩H]+E[⟨φF,DG⟩H]-E[⟨DF,φ⟩HG]=E[δ(φF)G].Since this is true for all cylinder functions G, the claimed identity follows. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

This gives a nice expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_0 F$$\end{document}L0F:

Corollary 2.3

For any cylinder function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \mathcal {C}$$\end{document}F∈C, we have15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}_0 F = \delta (- A D F) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L0F=δ(-ADF).

Proof

In (12) we can write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sum _{i, j = 1}^m\! \partial _{i j}^2 \Phi (\eta (f)) \langle A f_i, f_j \rangle _H\! \!=\! \!\sum _{i = 1}^m \!\langle D \partial _i \Phi (\eta (f)), A f_i \rangle _H $$\end{document}∑i,j=1m∂ij2Φ(η(f))⟨Afi,fj⟩H=∑i=1m⟨D∂iΦ(η(f)),Afi⟩H and therefore\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}_0 F (\eta )&= \sum _{i = 1}^m \partial _i \Phi (\eta (f)) \eta (- A f_i) + \sum _{i = 1}^m \langle D \partial _i \Phi (\eta (f)), A f_i \rangle _H\\&= \delta \left( \sum _{i = 1}^m \partial _i \Phi (\eta (f)) (- A f_i) \right) (\eta ) = \delta (- A D F) (\eta ) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L0F(η)=∑i=1m∂iΦ(η(f))η(-Afi)+∑i=1m⟨D∂iΦ(η(f)),Afi⟩H=δ∑i=1m∂iΦ(η(f))(-Afi)(η)=δ(-ADF)(η).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Our next goal is to derive an expression for the generator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}G corresponding to the nonlinearity B. Here we have to use a suitable approximation, and even the definition of the approximation takes some work.

Lemma 2.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho : H \rightarrow V$$\end{document}ρ:H→V be a bounded operator and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in H$$\end{document}v∈H. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(e_k)_k$$\end{document}(ek)k be an orthonormal basis of H. Then the following limits exist in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2 (\mu )$$\end{document}L2(μ) and do not depend on the specific orthonormal basis:16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle {:} B^{\rho } (\eta , \eta ) {:}, v \rangle _{V^{*}, V} :=\lim _{N \rightarrow \infty } \sum _{k, \ell \leqslant N} (\eta (e_k) \eta (e_{\ell }) - \delta _{k, \ell }) \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho v \rangle _{V^{*}, V} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨:Bρ(η,η):,v⟩V∗,V:=limN→∞∑k,ℓ⩽N(η(ek)η(eℓ)-δk,ℓ)⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρv⟩V∗,Vand, if additionally \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in V$$\end{document}v∈V, the same is true for17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle {:} \tilde{B}^{\rho } (\eta , \eta ) {:}, v \rangle _{V^{*}, V} :=\lim _{N \rightarrow \infty } \sum _{k, \ell \leqslant N} (\eta (e_k) \eta (e_{\ell }) - \delta _{k, \ell }) \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), v \rangle _{V^{*}, V}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨:B~ρ(η,η):,v⟩V∗,V:=limN→∞∑k,ℓ⩽N(η(ek)η(eℓ)-δk,ℓ)⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),v⟩V∗,V.

Proof

The term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{k, \ell \leqslant N} (\eta (e_k) \eta (e_{\ell }) - \delta _{k, \ell }) \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho v \rangle _{V^{*}, V}$$\end{document}∑k,ℓ⩽N(η(ek)η(eℓ)-δk,ℓ)⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρv⟩V∗,V is in the second homogeneous chaos, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (e_k) \eta (e_{\ell }) - \delta _{k, \ell } = \delta (e_k \delta (e_{\ell }))$$\end{document}η(ek)η(eℓ)-δk,ℓ=δ(ekδ(eℓ)) by Lemma 2.2. Consider a cylinder function G that is also in the second homogeneous chaos (this is sufficient for the expectation below, because any other chaos would give an orthogonal contribution to our sum). Integrating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ by parts twice, we obtain with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _N h = \sum _{k \leqslant N} \langle h, e_k \rangle _{V,V^{*}} e_k$$\end{document}ΠNh=∑k⩽N⟨h,ek⟩V,V∗ek\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathbb {E} \left[ \left( \sum _{k, \ell \leqslant N} (\eta (e_k) \eta (e_{\ell }) - \delta _{k, \ell }) \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho v \rangle _{V^{*}, V} \right) G \right] \\&= \sum _{k, \ell \leqslant N} \mathbb {E} [D_{e_k} D_{e_{\ell }} G \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho v \rangle _{V^{*}, V}]\\&=\mathbb {E} [\langle B (\rho ^{\otimes 2} \Pi _N^{\otimes 2} D^2 G), \rho v \rangle _{V^{*}, V}]\\&\lesssim \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (H, V)} \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (H, H)} \mathbb {E} [\Vert \Pi ^{\otimes 2}_N D^2 G \Vert _{H \otimes _s H}^2] \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (\dot{V}, \dot{V})} \Vert v \Vert _{\dot{V}}\\&\lesssim \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (H, V)} \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (H, H)} \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (\dot{V}, \dot{V})} \mathbb {E} [\Vert D^2 G \Vert _{H \otimes _s H}^2] \Vert v \Vert _{\dot{V}}\\&\simeq \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (H, V)} \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (H, H)} \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (\dot{V}, \dot{V})} {\Vert G \Vert _{L^2 (\mu )}} \Vert v \Vert _{\dot{V}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E∑k,ℓ⩽N(η(ek)η(eℓ)-δk,ℓ)⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρv⟩V∗,VG=∑k,ℓ⩽NE[DekDeℓG⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρv⟩V∗,V]=E[⟨B(ρ⊗2ΠN⊗2D2G),ρv⟩V∗,V]≲‖ρ‖L(H,V)‖ρ‖L(H,H)E[‖ΠN⊗2D2G‖H⊗sH2]‖ρ‖L(V˙,V˙)‖v‖V˙≲‖ρ‖L(H,V)‖ρ‖L(H,H)‖ρ‖L(V˙,V˙)E[‖D2G‖H⊗sH2]‖v‖V˙≃‖ρ‖L(H,V)‖ρ‖L(H,H)‖ρ‖L(V˙,V˙)‖G‖L2(μ)‖v‖V˙,where in the last step we used that the Malliavin derivative is a bounded operator when acting on a fixed chaos. This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigg ( \sum _{k, \ell \leqslant N} (\eta (e_k) \eta (e_{\ell }) - \delta _{k, \ell }) \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho v \rangle _{V^{*}, V} \bigg )_N$$\end{document}(∑k,ℓ⩽N(η(ek)η(eℓ)-δk,ℓ)⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρv⟩V∗,V)N is uniformly bounded in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2 (\mu )$$\end{document}L2(μ), and a similar argument using that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _N^{\otimes 2}$$\end{document}ΠN⊗2 approximates the identity in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H \otimes _s H$$\end{document}H⊗sH shows that it is a Cauchy sequence and its limit does not depend on the specific orthonormal basis used for the construction. The same argument works also for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${:} \tilde{B}^{\rho } (\eta , \eta ) {:}$$\end{document}:B~ρ(η,η): in place of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${:} B^{\rho } (\eta , \eta ){ :}$$\end{document}:Bρ(η,η):. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 2.5

For later reference note that we can extract the following bounds from the proof:18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \langle {:} B^{\rho } (\eta , \eta ) {:}, v \rangle _{V^{*}, V}\Vert _{L^2(\mu )}&\lesssim \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (H, V)} \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (H, H)} \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (\dot{V}, \dot{V})} \Vert v \Vert _{\dot{V}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖⟨:Bρ(η,η):,v⟩V∗,V‖L2(μ)≲‖ρ‖L(H,V)‖ρ‖L(H,H)‖ρ‖L(V˙,V˙)‖v‖V˙,19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \langle {:} \tilde{B}^{\rho } (\eta , \eta ) {:}, v \rangle _{V^{*}, V}\Vert _{L^2(\mu )}&\lesssim \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (H, V)} \Vert \rho \Vert _{L (H, H)} \Vert v \Vert _{\dot{V}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖⟨:B~ρ(η,η):,v⟩V∗,V‖L2(μ)≲‖ρ‖L(H,V)‖ρ‖L(H,H)‖v‖V˙.

Lemma 2.6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho : H \rightarrow V$$\end{document}ρ:H→V be a bounded operator. We define for cylinder functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F = \Phi (\eta (f)) \in \mathcal {C}$$\end{document}F=Φ(η(f))∈C with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {:} B^{\rho } (\eta , \eta ) {:}, f_i \rangle _{V^{*}, V}$$\end{document}⟨:Bρ(η,η):,fi⟩V∗,V as in (16)20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {G}^{\rho } F (\eta ) :=\sum _{i = 1}^m \partial _i \Phi (\eta (f)) \langle {:} B^{\rho } (\eta , \eta ) {:}, f_i \rangle _{V^{*}, V} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}GρF(η):=∑i=1m∂iΦ(η(f))⟨:Bρ(η,η):,fi⟩V∗,V.Then we can decompose21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} orthonormal basis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G+ρF=∑k,ℓ∈Nδ(eℓδ(ek⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDF⟩V∗,V)),23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {G}_-^{\rho } F&= 2 \sum _{k, \ell \in \mathbb {N}} \delta (e_k \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D D_{e_{\ell }} F \rangle _{V^{*}, V}) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G-ρF=2∑k,ℓ∈Nδ(ek⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDDeℓF⟩V∗,V).

Proof

By definition,24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {G}^{\rho } F (\eta ) = \sum _{k, \ell \in \mathbb {N}} \sum _{i = 1}^m \partial _i \Phi (\eta (f)) \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho f_i \rangle _{V^{*}, V} (\eta (e_k) \eta (e_{\ell }) - \delta _{k, \ell }) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}GρF(η)=∑k,ℓ∈N∑i=1m∂iΦ(η(f))⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρfi⟩V∗,V(η(ek)η(eℓ)-δk,ℓ)By the multiplication rule for Skorokhod integrals in Lemma 2.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (e_k) \eta (e_{\ell }) - \delta _{k, \ell } = \delta (\delta (e_k) e_{\ell }) (\eta )$$\end{document}η(ek)η(eℓ)-δk,ℓ=δ(δ(ek)eℓ)(η). Using once more multiplication rule in (13) in the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G \delta (\varphi ) = \delta (G \varphi ) + \langle D G, \varphi \rangle _H = \delta (G \varphi ) + D_{\varphi } G$$\end{document}Gδ(φ)=δ(Gφ)+⟨DG,φ⟩H=δ(Gφ)+DφG, we can thus rewrite \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}^{\rho } F$$\end{document}GρF as25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {G}^{\rho } F&= \sum _{k, \ell \in \mathbb {N}} \delta (\langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D F \rangle _{V^{*}, V} \delta (e_k) e_{\ell })\nonumber \\&\quad + \sum _{k, \ell \in \mathbb {N}} \langle D (\langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D F \rangle _{V^{*}, V}), e_{\ell } \delta (e_k) \rangle _H \nonumber \\&= \sum _{k, \ell } \delta (e_k) \nonumber \\&= \sum _{k, \ell } \delta (e_{\ell } \delta (e_k \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D F \rangle _{V^{*}, V})) + \sum _{k, \ell } \delta (e_{\ell } \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D D_{e_k} F \rangle _{V^{*}, V})\nonumber \\&\qquad + \sum _{k, \ell } \delta (e_k \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D D_{e_{\ell }} F \rangle _{V^{*}, V}) + \sum _{k, \ell } \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D D_{e_k} D_{e_{\ell }} F \rangle _{V^{*}, V} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}GρF=∑k,ℓ∈Nδ(⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDF⟩V∗,Vδ(ek)eℓ)+∑k,ℓ∈N⟨D(⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDF⟩V∗,V),eℓδ(ek)⟩H=∑k,ℓδ(eℓ⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDF⟩V∗,Vδ(ek))+∑k,ℓ⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDDeℓF⟩V∗,Vδ(ek)=∑k,ℓδ(eℓδ(ek⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDF⟩V∗,V))+∑k,ℓδ(eℓ⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDDekF⟩V∗,V)+∑k,ℓδ(ek⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDDeℓF⟩V∗,V)+∑k,ℓ⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDDekDeℓF⟩V∗,V.Expanding the third order Malliavin derivative in the last term on the right hand side, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{k, \ell } \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D D_{e_k} D_{e_{\ell }} F \rangle _{V^{*}, V}&= \sum _{i_1, i_2, i_3 = 1}^m \partial _{i_1 i_2 i_3}^3 \Phi (\eta (f)) \langle B (\rho f_{i_2}, \rho f_{i_3}), \rho f_{i_1} \rangle _{V^{*}, V} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑k,ℓ⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDDekDeℓF⟩V∗,V=∑i1,i2,i3=1m∂i1i2i33Φ(η(f))⟨B(ρfi2,ρfi3),ρfi1⟩V∗,V.To proceed, we need Lemma A.1 from the appendix, which shows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle B (\rho f_{i_2}, \rho f_{i_3}), \rho f_{i_1} \rangle _{V^{*}, V} + \langle B (\rho f_{i_1}, \rho f_{i_2}), \rho f_{i_3} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨B(ρfi2,ρfi3),ρfi1⟩V∗,V+⟨B(ρfi1,ρfi2),ρfi3⟩V∗,V+⟨B(ρfi3,ρfi1),ρfi2⟩V∗,V=0,and consequently, using the symmetry of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{i_1 i_2 i_3}^3 \Phi (\eta (f))$$\end{document}∂i1i2i33Φ(η(f)) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_1, i_2, i_3$$\end{document}i1,i2,i3,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{i_1, i_2, i_3 = 1}^m \partial _{i_1 i_2 i_3}^3 \Phi (\eta (f)) \langle B (\rho f_{i_2}, \rho f_{i_3}), \rho f_{i_1} \rangle _{V^{*}, V} = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑i1,i2,i3=1m∂i1i2i33Φ(η(f))⟨B(ρfi2,ρfi3),ρfi1⟩V∗,V=0.We remain with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {G}^{\rho } F&= \sum _{k, \ell } \delta (e_{\ell } \delta (\langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D F \rangle _{V^{*}, V} e_k))\\ &\quad + 2 \sum _{k, \ell } \delta (e_k (\langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D D_{e_{\ell }} F \rangle _{V^{*}, V}))\\&= \mathcal {G}^{\rho }_+ F +\mathcal {G}^{\rho }_- F, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}GρF=∑k,ℓδ(eℓδ(⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDF⟩V∗,Vek))+2∑k,ℓδ(ek(⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDDeℓF⟩V∗,V))=G+ρF+G-ρF,where we used the symmetry of B in the first line. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_+^{\rho }$$\end{document}G+ρ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_-^{\rho }$$\end{document}G-ρ are antisymmetric with respect to each other:

Lemma 2.7

If F and G are cylinder functions, then26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} [(\mathcal {G}_+^{\rho } F) G] = -\mathbb {E} [F\mathcal {G}^{\rho }_- G] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[(G+ρF)G]=-E[FG-ρG].

Proof

We use several times that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ and D are adjoint operators, and in the fifth line we use Lemma A.1 from the appendix together with the symmetry of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{e_k} D_{e_{\ell }} G$$\end{document}DekDeℓG in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k, \ell $$\end{document}k,ℓ27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} [(\mathcal {G}_+^{\rho } F) G]&= \sum _{k, \ell } \mathbb {E} [\delta (e_{\ell } \delta (e_k \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D F \rangle _{V^{*}, V})) G] \nonumber \\&= \sum _{k, \ell } \mathbb {E} [\delta (e_k \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D F \rangle _{V^{*}, V}) D_{e_{\ell }} G] \nonumber \\&= \sum _{k, \ell } \mathbb {E} [\langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho D F \rangle _{V^{*}, V} D_{e_k} D_{e_{\ell }} G] \\&= \sum _{k, \ell , m} \mathbb {E} [\langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_{\ell }), \rho e_m \rangle _{V^{*}, V} D_{e_m} F D_{e_k} D_{e_{\ell }} G] \nonumber \\&= - 2 \sum _{k, \ell , m} \mathbb {E} [\langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_m), \rho e_{\ell } \rangle _{V^{*}, V} D_{e_m} F D_{e_k} D_{e_{\ell }} G] \nonumber \\&= - 2 \sum _{k, m} \mathbb {E} [\langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_m), \rho D D_{e_k} G \rangle _{V^{*}, V} D_{e_m} F] \nonumber \\&= - 2 \sum _{k, m \leqslant N} \mathbb {E} [\delta (e_m \langle B (\rho e_k, \rho e_m), \rho D D_{e_k} G \rangle _{V^{*}, V}) F] \nonumber \\&= -\mathbb {E} [F\mathcal {G}_- G] . \nonumber \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[(G+ρF)G]=∑k,ℓE[δ(eℓδ(ek⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDF⟩V∗,V))G]=∑k,ℓE[δ(ek⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDF⟩V∗,V)DeℓG]=∑k,ℓE[⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρDF⟩V∗,VDekDeℓG]=∑k,ℓ,mE[⟨B(ρek,ρeℓ),ρem⟩V∗,VDemFDekDeℓG]=-2∑k,ℓ,mE[⟨B(ρek,ρem),ρeℓ⟩V∗,VDemFDekDeℓG]=-2∑k,mE[⟨B(ρek,ρem),ρDDekG⟩V∗,VDemF]=-2∑k,m⩽NE[δ(em⟨B(ρek,ρem),ρDDekG⟩V∗,V)F]=-E[FG-G].\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 2.8

For later reference, we note that equation (27) can be written as28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} [(\mathcal {G}_+^{\rho } F) G] = \mathbb {E} [\langle B (\rho ^{\otimes 2} D^2G), \rho D F \rangle _{V^{*}, V}]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[(G+ρF)G]=E[⟨B(ρ⊗2D2G),ρDF⟩V∗,V].

To derive estimates for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G^\rho }$$\end{document}Gρ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_0$$\end{document}L0, we need to introduce suitable function spaces.

Function spaces and operator bounds

Here we define Sobolev type spaces over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2 (\mu )$$\end{document}L2(μ), which can be interpreted as tensorizations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V, H, V^{*}$$\end{document}V,H,V∗ and also of the homogeneous spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{V}, \dot{V}^{*}$$\end{document}V˙,V˙∗. We start with the definition of an auxiliary operator.

Definition 2.9

(Number operator/Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operator) The number operator, also called Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operator, is defined as29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {N} :=\delta D, \qquad \operatorname {dom} (\mathcal {N}) :=\{ F \in \operatorname {dom} (D) : D F \in \operatorname {dom} (\delta ) \} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N:=δD,dom(N):={F∈dom(D):DF∈dom(δ)}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}$$\end{document}N is a self-adjoint, positive semi-definite linear operator, see [32, p. 59], and therefore we can define the powers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1 +\mathcal {N})^{\alpha }$$\end{document}(1+N)α using functional calculus. We have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C} \subset \operatorname {dom} ((1 +\mathcal {N})^{\alpha })$$\end{document}C⊂dom((1+N)α) for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}α∈R, and in fact \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1 +\mathcal {N})^{\alpha } \mathcal {C} \subset \mathcal {C}$$\end{document}(1+N)αC⊂C. This can be shown using the chaos expansion under the Gaussian measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}$$\end{document}N acts as multiplication with n on the n-th homogeneous chaos, and any element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C has a finite chaos expansion.

Lemma 2.10

The operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_0$$\end{document}L0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}$$\end{document}N commute on cylinder functions:30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathcal {L}_0, \mathcal {N}] F = \mathcal {L}_0 \mathcal {N} F - \mathcal {N} \mathcal {L}_0 F = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[L0,N]F=L0NF-NL0F=0,for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \mathcal {C}$$\end{document}F∈C.

Proof

By Lemma A.2, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal {L}_0 \mathcal {N} F = - \delta (A D \mathcal {N} F) = - \delta (A (\mathcal {N} + 1) D \mathcal F) = - \delta ((\mathcal {N} + 1) A D \mathcal F) = - \mathcal {N} \delta (A D \mathcal F) . $$\end{document}L0NF=-δ(ADNF)=-δ(A(N+1)DF)=-δ((N+1)ADF)=-Nδ(ADF).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

For any cylinder function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} The homogeneous spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{\mathcal {H}}^1_0$$\end{document}H˙01 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{\mathcal {H}}^{-1}_0$$\end{document}H˙0-1 play an important role when studying long time fluctuations in Markov processes, see the monograph [28]. Lemma 2.12

(Bounds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_0$$\end{document}L0) The operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_0$$\end{document}L0 to a bounded linear operator from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek}
