f\Vert _{\dot{V}}^2 \simeq \sum _{k>N} \lambda _k \langle f, e_k\rangle _H^2 \leqslant \lambda _N^{-1}\sum _{k>N} \lambda _k^2 \langle f, e_k\rangle _H^2 = \lambda _N^{-1} \Vert (1 - \Pi _N) Af\Vert _H^2. $$\end{document}‖ΠNf-f‖V˙2≃∑k>Nλk⟨f,ek⟩H2⩽λN-1∑k>Nλk2⟨f,ek⟩H2=λN-1‖(1-ΠN)Af‖H2.By the Besov-variation embedding already used in Lemma 2.20, we get for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p<2$$\end{document}p<268\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{N \rightarrow \infty } \mathbb {E}_{\mu ^N} \left[ \left\| \int _0^{\cdot } \langle {:}B(\Pi _N u^N_s,\Pi _N u^N_s){:}, \Pi _N f - f\rangle _{V^*, V} \textrm{d}s \right\| _{[0, T], p - \textrm{var}}^2 \right] ^{1/2} = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→∞EμN∫0·⟨:B(ΠNusN,ΠNusN):,ΠNf-f⟩V∗,Vds[0,T],p-var21/2=0.For the remaining term in (67) we use the same interpolation argument as in the proof of Lemma 2.20 together with the bound \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \Pi _N^{\otimes 2}\Vert _{L(V\otimes _s H, V\otimes _s H)} \lesssim 1$$\end{document}‖ΠN⊗2‖L(V⊗sH,V⊗sH)≲1 from Lemma A.3 in the appendix, to obtain a bound on the increment:69\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}_{\mu ^N}\left[ \left| \int _s^t \langle {:}B(\Pi _N u^N_r,\Pi _N u^N_r){:} -{:}{\tilde{B}}^{\rho _M}(\Pi _N u^N_r,\Pi _N u^N_r){:}, f\rangle _{V^*, V} \textrm{d}r\right| ^2\right] \lesssim \varepsilon _M^\beta (t-s)^{1+\zeta } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EμN∫st⟨:B(ΠNurN,ΠNurN):-:B~ρM(ΠNurN,ΠNurN):,f⟩V∗,Vdr2≲εMβ(t-s)1+ζfor some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ,\zeta >0$$\end{document}β,ζ>0. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p<2$$\end{document}p<2 is such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2/p < 1+\zeta $$\end{document}2/p<1+ζ and such that there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p'\in (p,2)$$\end{document}p′∈(p,2) for which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( \Vert \mathcal {B}^{f,N} \Vert _{[0, T], p' - \operatorname {var}})_N$$\end{document}(‖Bf,N‖[0,T],p′-var)N is tight, we combine (67), (68), (69) and we use the same Besov-variation embedding as in Lemma 2.20 to deduce (66). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Uniqueness proof: Duality of semigroup and energy solutions

To prove the uniqueness of energy solutions, we will show that every energy solution solves the martingale problem for the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\mathcal {D}}_{\textrm{max}}, {\mathcal {L}})$$\end{document}(Dmax,L) constructed in Proposition 3.1 and Lemma 3.2. For that purpose, we first extend the weak solution property from linear test functions to cylinder functions.

Lemma 3.6

(Itô formula) Let u be an energy solution and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \mathcal {C}$$\end{document}F∈C. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(u_t) - F(u_0) - I (\mathcal {L}F)_t, \qquad t \geqslant 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(ut)-F(u0)-I(LF)t,t⩾0,is a continuous martingale.

Proof

This follows by the same arguments as [21, Lem. 4.14], relying on Itô’s formula, Young integration and the convergence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _0^\cdot \langle {:} {\tilde{B}}^{\rho _N} (u_s, u_s) {:}, f \rangle _{V^{*}, V} \textrm{d}s$$\end{document}∫0·⟨:B~ρN(us,us):,f⟩V∗,Vds to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I (\langle {:} B (\cdot , \cdot ) {:}, f \rangle _{V^{*}, V})$$\end{document}I(⟨:B(·,·):,f⟩V∗,V) in p-variation. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Theorem 3.7

(Uniqueness of energy solutions) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu \ll \mu $$\end{document}ν≪μ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\textrm{d}\nu }{\textrm{d}\mu } \in L^2 (\mu )$$\end{document}dνdμ∈L2(μ) and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_t)_{t \geqslant 0}$$\end{document}(ut)t⩾0 be an energy solution with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0 \sim \nu $$\end{document}u0∼ν. Then u is a solution to the martingale problem for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {D}_{\max }, \mathcal {L})$$\end{document}(Dmax,L), i.e. for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \mathcal {D}_{\max }$$\end{document}F∈Dmax it holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ F (u_t) - F (u_0) - \int _0^t \mathcal {L} F (u_s) \textrm{d}s, \qquad t \geqslant 0, $$\end{document}F(ut)-F(u0)-∫0tLF(us)ds,t⩾0,is a martingale in the filtration generated by u. Consequently, the law of u is uniquely determined by its initial condition, it is a Markov process and it has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ as an invariant measure.

Proof

To see that u is a solution to the martingale problem for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {D}_{\max }, \mathcal {L})$$\end{document}(Dmax,L), let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \mathcal {D}_{\max }$$\end{document}F∈Dmax. By Lemma 3.2, there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^{\sharp } \in L^2 (\mu )$$\end{document}F♯∈L2(μ) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F =\mathcal {R}_1 F^{\sharp }$$\end{document}F=R1F♯, and again by Lemma 3.2 the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1 -\mathcal {L}) \mathcal {C}$$\end{document}(1-L)C is dense in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^{- 1}_0$$\end{document}H0-1. Therefore, there exists a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(F^N)_N \subset \mathcal {C}$$\end{document}(FN)N⊂C with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1 -\mathcal {L}) F^N \rightarrow F^{\sharp }$$\end{document}(1-L)FN→F♯ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^{- 1}_0$$\end{document}H0-1. By Proposition 3.1 we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}_1 \in L (\mathcal {H}^{- 1}_0, \mathcal {H}^1_0)$$\end{document}R1∈L(H0-1,H01), and therefore the following convergence holds in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^1_0$$\end{document}H01:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ F^N =\mathcal {R}_1 (1 -\mathcal {L}) F^N \rightarrow \mathcal {R}_1 F^{\sharp } = F, $$\end{document}FN=R1(1-L)FN→R1F♯=F,where the first equality follows from the uniqueness of solutions to the resolvent equation in Lemma 3.2, as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (1 -\mathcal {L}) F^N = (1 -\mathcal {L}) (\mathcal {R}_1 (1 -\mathcal {L}) F^N) . $$\end{document}(1-L)FN=(1-L)(R1(1-L)FN).We now use this convergence to justify that u solves the martingale problem for the test function F. By the Itô formula in Lemma 3.6, the process\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F^N (u_t) - F^N (u_0) - I (\mathcal {L}F^N)_t, \qquad t \geqslant 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FN(ut)-FN(u0)-I(LFN)t,t⩾0,is a continuous martingale, and we will pass to the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}N→∞ in each term. First observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^N (u_t) - F^N (u_0) \rightarrow F (u_t) - F (u_0)$$\end{document}FN(ut)-FN(u0)→F(ut)-F(u0) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1 (\mathbb {P})$$\end{document}L1(P), since by the uniform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 bound for the density ii.,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |\mathbb {E}[(F - F^N) (u_t) - (F - F^N) (u_0)] | \lesssim \Vert F - F^N \Vert _{L^2 (\mu )} \lesssim \Vert F - F^N \Vert _{\mathcal {H}^1_0} \rightarrow 0. $$\end{document}|E[(F-FN)(ut)-(F-FN)(u0)]|≲‖F-FN‖L2(μ)≲‖F-FN‖H01→0.For the integral term, we use that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1 -\mathcal {L}) F = F^{\sharp }$$\end{document}(1-L)F=F♯ and combine this with the energy estimate iii. to obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} [|I (\mathcal {L}(F - F^N))_t |]&=\mathbb {E} [|I ((F - F^N) - (F^{\sharp } - (1 -\mathcal {L}) F^N))_t |]\\&\lesssim \Vert (F - F^N) - (F^{\sharp } - (1 -\mathcal {L}) F^N)\Vert _{\mathcal {H}^{- 1}_0} \rightarrow 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[|I(L(F-FN))t|]=E[|I((F-FN)-(F♯-(1-L)FN))t|]≲‖(F-FN)-(F♯-(1-L)FN)‖H0-1→0.The martingale property is preserved under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1$$\end{document}L1-limits and thus u is a solution to the martingale problem for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {D}_{\max }, \mathcal {L})$$\end{document}(Dmax,L).

Now uniqueness of u, Markov property and invariance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ follow by the same arguments as in [21, Theorem 4.8]: The martingale problem extends to time-dependent test functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in C^1_T L^2 (\mu ) \cap C_T \mathcal {D}_{\max }$$\end{document}F∈CT1L2(μ)∩CTDmax by writing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F (t, u_t) - F (0, u_0) = \sum _{k = 0}^{n - 1} (F (t_{k + 1}^n, u_{t^n_{k + 1}}) - F (t^n_k, u_{t^n_k}))$$\end{document}F(t,ut)-F(0,u0)=∑k=0n-1(F(tk+1n,utk+1n)-F(tkn,utkn)) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t^n_k = k t / n$$\end{document}tkn=kt/n, and using a Taylor expansion of F in t, and the martingale problem for time-independent test functions in the u variable. This shows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ F (t, u_t) - F (0, u_0) - \int _0^t (\partial _s +\mathcal {L}) F (s, u_s) \textrm{d}s, \qquad t \in [0, T], $$\end{document}F(t,ut)-F(0,u0)-∫0t(∂s+L)F(s,us)ds,t∈[0,T],is a continuous martingale, and in particular it has vanishing expectation. Now it suffices to take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F (t, u) = P_{T - t} G (u)$$\end{document}F(t,u)=PT-tG(u) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G \in \mathcal {D}_{\max }$$\end{document}G∈Dmax and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(P_t)_{t \geqslant 0}$$\end{document}(Pt)t⩾0 the semigroup from Proposition 3.1, which leads to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} [G (u_T)] =\mathbb {E} [P_T G (u_0)], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[G(uT)]=E[PTG(u0)],so we obtain the uniqueness of one-dimensional marginals. The usual iteration as in [12, Theorem 4.4.2] or [21, Theorem 4.8] then yields uniqueness of the finite-dimensional distributions. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 3.8

(Ergodicity of energy solutions) Assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v = 0$$\end{document}v=0 is the only element of V with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert v \Vert _{\dot{V}} = 0$$\end{document}‖v‖V˙=0. Then the invariant measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ is ergodic for the Markov process u from the previous theorem. Indeed, it suffices to show that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \mathcal {D}_{\max }$$\end{document}F∈Dmax solves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}F = 0$$\end{document}LF=0, then F is a.s. constant. But we know from Lemma 3.2 that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 0 = \langle -\mathcal {L}F, F \rangle _{\mathcal {H}^{- 1}_0, \mathcal {H}^1_0} = \langle -\mathcal {L}_0 F, F \rangle _{\mathcal {H}^{- 1}_0, \mathcal {H}^1_0} \simeq \mathbb {E} [\Vert D F \Vert _{\dot{V}}^2], $$\end{document}0=⟨-LF,F⟩H0-1,H01=⟨-L0F,F⟩H0-1,H01≃E[‖DF‖V˙2],so by assumption \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D F = 0$$\end{document}DF=0 a.s. and thus F is a.s. constant.

Examples

Here we discuss in detail the examples mentioned in the introduction. In each example we identify \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H,V,A,B,(\rho _N)$$\end{document}H,V,A,B,(ρN) and verify the conditions in Assumptions 1 and 1. For Assumption 1 we do not explicitly verify that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert B (v, w) \Vert _{\dot{V}^{*}} \lesssim \Vert v\Vert _V \Vert w\Vert _H + \Vert v\Vert _H \Vert w\Vert _V$$\end{document}‖B(v,w)‖V˙∗≲‖v‖V‖w‖H+‖v‖H‖w‖V, and instead we only show the stronger condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert B (\varphi ) \Vert _{\dot{V}^{*}} \lesssim \Vert \varphi \Vert _{V \otimes _s H}$$\end{document}‖B(φ)‖V˙∗≲‖φ‖V⊗sH, which controls also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B(v,w) = B (v \otimes w)$$\end{document}B(v,w)=B(v⊗w).

Example 4.1

(Multi-component Burgers equation with Dirichlet boundary conditions on (0, 1) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0, \infty ))$$\end{document}(0,∞)) Consider the multi-component stochastic Burgers equation on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D \in \{ (0, 1), (0, \infty ) \}$$\end{document}D∈{(0,1),(0,∞)} with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions: Formally, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u: \mathbb {R}_+ \times D \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}u:R+×D→Rd with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \partial _t u^i = \Delta u^i + {: }\sum _{j, k} \Gamma ^i_{j k} \partial _x (u^j u^k) {:} + \sqrt{2 (- \Delta )} \xi ^i, $$\end{document}∂tui=Δui+:∑j,kΓjki∂x(ujuk):+2(-Δ)ξi,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ is symmetric in its 3 arguments. We choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H = L^2 (D \times \{ 1, \ldots , d \}) = L^2 (D)^d$$\end{document}H=L2(D×{1,…,d})=L2(D)d, the smooth functions are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ S = C^{\infty }_c (D \times \{ 1, \ldots , d \}) = C^{\infty }_c (D)^d$$\end{document}S=Cc∞(D×{1,…,d})=Cc∞(D)d, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V = H^1_0 (D \times \{ 1, \ldots , d \}) = H^1_0 (D)^d$$\end{document}V=H01(D×{1,…,d})=H01(D)d with norm\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert v \Vert _V^2 = \sum _j (\Vert v^j \Vert _{L^2}^2 + \Vert \partial _x v^j \Vert _{L^2}^2), $$\end{document}‖v‖V2=∑j(‖vj‖L22+‖∂xvj‖L22),and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert v \Vert _{\dot{V}}^2 = \Vert v \Vert _V^2 - \Vert v \Vert _H^2 = \sum _j \Vert \partial _x v^j \Vert _{L^2}^2$$\end{document}‖v‖V˙2=‖v‖V2-‖v‖H2=∑j‖∂xvj‖L22.

We define approximations similarly to [25] by averaging over a rescaled indicator function with ”slowed" shifting near the boundary in order to stay inside of the domain. Moreover, using the symmetry of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ in its arguments i, j, k and integration by parts, we see that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in S$$\end{document}f∈S\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle B (f, f), f \rangle _{V^{*}, V} = \sum _{i, j, k} \Gamma ^i_{j k} \int _D \partial _x (f^j (x) f^k (x)) f^i (x) \textrm{d}x = 0, \qquad f \in S. $$\end{document}⟨B(f,f),f⟩V∗,V=∑i,j,kΓjki∫D∂x(fj(x)fk(x))fi(x)dx=0,f∈S.Our remaining task is to verify that the approximation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\rho _N)$$\end{document}(ρN) has the required properties. We know from Lemma A.5 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sup _N \Vert \rho _N \Vert _{L (H, H)} + \Vert \rho _N \Vert _{L (\dot{V}, \dot{V})} < \infty $$\end{document}supN‖ρN‖L(H,H)+‖ρN‖L(V˙,V˙)<∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \rho _N\Vert _{L(H,V)} \lesssim N$$\end{document}‖ρN‖L(H,V)≲N, and for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in S$$\end{document}f∈S and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in {\mathcal {C}}^\infty _c(D^2)$$\end{document}φ∈Cc∞(D2) we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \rho _N f - f \Vert _V&\lesssim N^{-1} \Vert f\Vert _{H^2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖ρNf-f‖V≲N-1‖f‖H2,as well as by (72) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =0$$\end{document}α=0, Lemma A.5 and (73),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle B(\varphi ),(\rho _N-\text {Id})f\rangle _{V^*,V} \lesssim \Vert \varphi \Vert _{H^{3/2}(D^2)}\Vert (\rho _N-\text {Id})f\Vert _{L^2}\lesssim N^{-1}\Vert \varphi \Vert _{H^{3/2}(D^2)}\Vert f\Vert _{H^1} , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨B(φ),(ρN-Id)f⟩V∗,V≲‖φ‖H3/2(D2)‖(ρN-Id)f‖L2≲N-1‖φ‖H3/2(D2)‖f‖H1, and also for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \in (0,\tfrac{1}{2})$$\end{document}κ∈(0,12),74\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} | \langle B(\rho _N^{\otimes 2} \varphi -\varphi ), f \rangle _{V^{*}, V} |&\lesssim \Vert \rho _N^{\otimes 2} \varphi -\varphi \Vert _{H^{1/2+\kappa }(D^2)} \Vert f\Vert _{\dot{H}^{1-\kappa }}\nonumber \\ &\lesssim N^{\kappa -1/2}\Vert \varphi \Vert _{H^1(D^2)}\Vert f\Vert _{H^{1}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|⟨B(ρN⊗2φ-φ),f⟩V∗,V|≲‖ρN⊗2φ-φ‖H1/2+κ(D2)‖f‖H˙1-κ≲Nκ-1/2‖φ‖H1(D2)‖f‖H1,where the last step follows by another application of Lemma A.5 together with a tensorization argument. Therefore, Assumptions 1 and 1 are satisfied in this example. The conditions for our existence theorem are satisfied if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D=(0,1)$$\end{document}D=(0,1) (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_k(x) = \sqrt{2} \sin (k\pi x)$$\end{document}ek(x)=2sin(kπx)), but not for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D=(0,\infty )$$\end{document}D=(0,∞).

Remark 4.2

Although uniqueness holds on non-compact domains such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,\infty )$$\end{document}(0,∞) (since it only relies on Assumptions (A) and (B)), the existence result of Theorem 3.5 does not apply in that setting because here we assume that A admits an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors, which fails for the Laplacian on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,\infty )$$\end{document}(0,∞). See Remark 1.7 for a possible strategy that could prove the existence of energy solutions also in that case.

To simplify the presentation we restrict to scalar-valued equations from now on, but in all of the following examples it would also be possible to consider systems of equations.

Example 4.3

(Burgers equation with elliptic differential operator and Dirichlet Boundary conditions) We consider the same equation as before, again on a domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D \in \{ (0, 1), (0, \infty ) \}$$\end{document}D∈{(0,1),(0,∞)}, and for simplicity we assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d = 1$$\end{document}d=1 (scalar-valued equation). We change the operator to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A v = - \partial _x (a \partial _x v), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Av=-∂x(a∂xv),for a measurable function a such that there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon > 0$$\end{document}ε>0 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a (x) \in [\varepsilon , \varepsilon ^{- 1}]$$\end{document}a(x)∈[ε,ε-1] for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in D$$\end{document}x∈D. The spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H, S, V, \dot{V}$$\end{document}H,S,V,V˙ have the same definition as before, as does the approximation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\rho _N)$$\end{document}(ρN). Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f, g \in S$$\end{document}f,g∈S have compact support, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle A f, g \rangle _{V^*, V} = - \int _D \partial _x (a \partial _x f) (x) g (x) \textrm{d}x = \int _D a(x) \partial _x f (x) \partial _x g (x) \textrm{d}x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨Af,g⟩V∗,V=-∫D∂x(a∂xf)(x)g(x)dx=∫Da(x)∂xf(x)∂xg(x)dx,and thus\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\langle A f, g \rangle _{V^{*}, V} \leqslant \varepsilon ^{-1} \Vert \partial _x f \Vert _{L^2} \Vert \partial _x g \Vert _{L^2} \lesssim \Vert f \Vert _{\dot{V}} \Vert g \Vert _{\dot{V}}, \quad \langle A f, f \rangle _{V^{*}, V} \geqslant \varepsilon \Vert \partial _x f \Vert ^2_{L^2} = \varepsilon \Vert f \Vert _{\dot{V}}^2 . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨Af,g⟩V∗,V⩽ε-1‖∂xf‖L2‖∂xg‖L2≲‖f‖V˙‖g‖V˙,⟨Af,f⟩V∗,V⩾ε‖∂xf‖22=ε‖f‖V˙2.

Moreover, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta >1$$\end{document}β>1 we can bound \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \varphi \Vert _\infty \lesssim \Vert \varphi \Vert _{H^\beta }$$\end{document}‖φ‖∞≲‖φ‖Hβ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert f\Vert _\infty \lesssim \Vert f\Vert _{H^{\beta /2}}$$\end{document}‖f‖∞≲‖f‖Hβ/2, again by a spectral computation. Therefore, using that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta >1$$\end{document}θ>1, we can find a small \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon >0$$\end{document}ε>0 such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle B (\varphi ), f \rangle _{V^{*}, V}\lesssim \Vert \varphi \Vert _{H^{\theta -\varepsilon }} \Vert f \Vert _{H^{\theta -\varepsilon }}. $$\end{document}⟨B(φ),f⟩V∗,V≲‖φ‖Hθ-ε‖f‖Hθ-ε.Moreover, due to the boundary renormalization we obtain for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in S$$\end{document}f∈S\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle B (f, f), f \rangle _{V^{*}, V} = \int _0^1 \frac{2}{3} \partial _x f^3 (x) \textrm{d}x - \frac{2}{3} (f (1)^3 - f (0)^3) = 0. $$\end{document}⟨B(f,f),f⟩V∗,V=∫0123∂xf3(x)dx-23(f(1)3-f(0)3)=0.Another simple Fourier computation shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _N$$\end{document}ρN satisfies the same bounds as in Lemma A.5, actually for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\leqslant \alpha \leqslant \beta $$\end{document}0⩽α⩽β, without requiring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \leqslant 1$$\end{document}β⩽1. Therefore, the bounds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \rho _N f - f\Vert _{V} \lesssim N^{-\theta } \Vert f\Vert _{H^{2\theta }}$$\end{document}‖ρNf-f‖V≲N-θ‖f‖H2θ and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |\langle B (\rho _N^{\otimes 2}\varphi ), f \rangle _{V^{*}, V} - \langle B (\varphi ), f \rangle _{V^{*}, V}| \lesssim N^{-\varepsilon } \Vert \varphi \Vert _{V\otimes _s H} \Vert f\Vert _{\dot{V}} $$\end{document}|⟨B(ρN⊗2φ),f⟩V∗,V-⟨B(φ),f⟩V∗,V|≲N-ε‖φ‖V⊗sH‖f‖V˙follow as in Example 4.1.

We have shown that Assumptions 1 and 1 are satisfied in this example, and we already heavily used that A can be diagonalized, so that we also obtain the existence of energy solutions. Unfortunately we required \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta > 1$$\end{document}θ>1 both for estimating the integral part and the boundary part of B, and thus we are unable to solve the non-fractional stochastic Burgers equation with (renormalized) homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions.

Example 4.5

Finally, we obtain for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in S$$\end{document}f∈S\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle B (f, f), f \rangle _{V^{*}, V} = \int _D \partial _x (f (x) f (x)) f (x) \textrm{d}x = \frac{2}{3} \int _D \partial _x f^3 (x) \textrm{d}x = 0, \qquad v \in V. $$\end{document}⟨B(f,f),f⟩V∗,V=∫D∂x(f(x)f(x))f(x)dx=23∫D∂xf3(x)dx=0,v∈V.Therefore, Assumptions 1 and 1 are satisfied. Also, A is diagonalizable because V is compactly embedded in H, and therefore our existence proof applies. But actually the existence of energy solutions for this example was previously shown by Cardoso and Gonçalves [2], who derive them as scaling limits of fluctuations in long range interacting particle systems. They consider only the stationary case, but the derivation should extend without problems to initial conditions with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 density or even \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1$$\end{document}L1 density as in [23].

Apart from A being diagonalizable, everything should extend also to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,\infty )$$\end{document}(0,∞) instead of (0, 1).
